It just throws off the plan I’m used to, and I’d rather not deal with that.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel anxious or uncomfortable in social situations where eating is involved, like when your friends invite you out after book club?\n\nPatient: A little, yes. I mean… I wouldn’t call it a major problem. I keep up with work and errands. But after book club, if they want to get dinner, I usually don’t go now. It just throws off the plan I rely on.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever worry about gaining weight or feel a need to keep tight control over how much you eat?\n\nPatient: Yes… I suppose I do. If the scale is even up a pound after a normal dinner, or if I’ve eaten at a restaurant, I get this sudden panic feeling. Like I’ve let things get away from me. Then I feel I need to be more careful the next day.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself having upsetting thoughts that keep coming back, or do you feel the need to do certain things over and over to feel better?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure. I don’t think I have… rituals, exactly. I do tend to go over things in my head, but I wouldn’t know if that’s what you mean.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever have thoughts that come into your mind uninvited, that you don’t want to have, and that bother you a lot—even if you're not thinking about food or weight?\n\nPatient: Not really… not that I can think of. I mean, I worry, but I wouldn’t say thoughts just come in out of nowhere like that.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever find yourself doing things like counting calories repeatedly, checking your weight often, or following specific rules about food to manage your anxiety?\n\nPatient: I do have rules, I guess. I don’t think of it as… a ritual. It’s more that I keep breakfast to yogurt, and if I’m busy I’ll skip lunch. Then dinner is pretty measured out, just so the day feels orderly. My doctor seems more concerned about it than I am.\n\nDoctor: How do you feel about missing out on time with your friends after book club because of your eating plan? Does it bother you, or is it more of a relief to stick to your routine?\n\nPatient: I wouldn’t say it bothers me exactly. I still get my work done and run my errands and all that. But… yes, I’ve mostly stopped going along when they get a meal after book club. It just throws off the plan I count on, and sticking to it feels easier.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. I don’t think I have… rituals, exactly. I do tend to go over things in my head, but I wouldn’t know if that’s what you mean.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever have thoughts that come into your mind uninvited, that you don’t want to have, and that bother you a lot—even if you're not thinking about food or weight?\n\nPatient: Not really… not that I can think of. I mean, I worry, but I wouldn’t say thoughts just come in out of nowhere like that.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever find yourself doing things like counting calories repeatedly, checking your weight often, or following specific rules about food to manage your anxiety?\n\nPatient: I do have rules, I guess. I don’t think of it as… a ritual. It’s more that I keep breakfast to yogurt, and if I’m busy I’ll skip lunch. Then dinner is pretty measured out, just so the day feels orderly. My doctor seems more concerned about it than I am.\n\nDoctor: How do you feel about missing out on time with your friends after book club because of your eating plan? Does it bother you, or is it more of a relief to stick to your routine?\n\nPatient: I wouldn’t say it bothers me exactly. I still get my work done and run my errands and all that. But… yes, I’ve mostly stopped going along when they get a meal after book club. It just throws off the plan I count on, and sticking to it feels easier.\n\nDoctor: When you choose not to go out with your friends after book club, does it cause you any real sadness or discomfort, or do you mostly feel that it’s just easier to stay with your routine?\n\nPatient: Mostly it’s just easier to stick with my routine. I don’t feel… deeply sad about it. I still do my work and errands fine. I’ve just stopped going for meals after book club because it throws off the plan I rely on.\n\nDoctor: When you choose not to go out to eat with your friends after book club, do you feel that you're missing out on something important, or does it not really affect how you feel about being with others?\n\nPatient: I don’t think it changes how I feel about them, really. I like seeing them at book club. I just… don’t usually go for the meal afterward anymore. It interferes with the food plan I’m used to. I’m still managing work and errands and things.\n\nDoctor: When you decide not to go out to eat with your friends after book club, do you ever feel upset or anxious about missing time with them, or is it more of a relief to avoid the situation?\n\nPatient: It’s more of a relief, I suppose. I mean, I still keep up with my analysis work and errands. I just usually don’t join them for meals after book club because it throws off the plan I rely on.\n\nDoctor: When you choose not to go out to eat with your friends after book club, do you ever feel distant from them or worry that they might think you don’t enjoy their company anymore?\n\nPatient: I don’t really think about it that way. I still see them at book club, so… I don’t feel completely distant. I just don’t usually go along for the meal afterward anymore. It disrupts the plan I’m trying to keep. My work and errands are still fine.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Ask about exactly ONE thing. I don’t think I have… rituals, exactly. I do tend to go over things in my head, but I wouldn’t know if that’s what you mean.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever have thoughts that come into your mind uninvited, that you don’t want to have, and that bother you a lot—even if you're not thinking about food or weight?\n\nPatient: Not really… not that I can think of. I mean, I worry, but I wouldn’t say thoughts just come in out of nowhere like that.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever find yourself doing things like counting calories repeatedly, checking your weight often, or following specific rules about food to manage your anxiety?\n\nPatient: I do have rules, I guess. I don’t think of it as… a ritual. It’s more that I keep breakfast to yogurt, and if I’m busy I’ll skip lunch. Then dinner is pretty measured out, just so the day feels orderly. My doctor seems more concerned about it than I am.\n\nDoctor: How do you feel about missing out on time with your friends after book club because of your eating plan? Does it bother you, or is it more of a relief to stick to your routine?\n\nPatient: I wouldn’t say it bothers me exactly. I still get my work done and run my errands and all that. But… yes, I’ve mostly stopped going along when they get a meal after book club. It just throws off the plan I count on, and sticking to it feels easier.\n\nDoctor: When you choose not to go out with your friends after book club, does it cause you any real sadness or discomfort, or do you mostly feel that it’s just easier to stay with your routine?\n\nPatient: Mostly it’s just easier to stick with my routine. I don’t feel… deeply sad about it. I still do my work and errands fine. I’ve just stopped going for meals after book club because it throws off the plan I rely on.\n\nDoctor: When you choose not to go out to eat with your friends after book club, do you feel that you're missing out on something important, or does it not really affect how you feel about being with others?\n\nPatient: I don’t think it changes how I feel about them, really. I like seeing them at book club. I just… don’t usually go for the meal afterward anymore. It interferes with the food plan I’m used to. I’m still managing work and errands and things.\n\nDoctor: When you decide not to go out to eat with your friends after book club, do you ever feel upset or anxious about missing time with them, or is it more of a relief to avoid the situation?\n\nPatient: It’s more of a relief, I suppose. I mean, I still keep up with my analysis work and errands. I just usually don’t join them for meals after book club because it throws off the plan I rely on.\n\nDoctor: When you choose not to go out to eat with your friends after book club, do you ever feel distant from them or worry that they might think you don’t enjoy their company anymore?\n\nPatient: I don’t really think about it that way. I still see them at book club, so… I don’t feel completely distant. I just don’t usually go along for the meal afterward anymore. It disrupts the plan I’m trying to keep. My work and errands are still fine.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever feel that avoiding meals with your friends after book club makes it harder for you to connect with them or causes you to feel left out, even if it feels easier in the moment?\n\nPatient: Maybe a little, but I don’t dwell on it. I still see them at book club. I just… mostly don’t go to the meals after, because it throws off the plan I’m used to. Work and errands are still getting done.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever feel distressed or upset about not joining your friends for meals after book club, or is it something you accept because it helps you manage your eating routine?\n\nPatient: I guess I mostly accept it. I’m not… terribly upset about it. It just feels easier to keep to the routine. I still get my work done and manage my errands, but the meals after book club tend to throw off the plan I rely on, so I usually don’t go.\n\nDoctor: Outside of avoiding meals with friends after book club, have you noticed any other areas of your life where you feel restricted or limited because of your eating habits, such as difficulties with work, family responsibilities, or other social activities?\n\nPatient: Not really in work or errands, no. I still keep up with my analysis work and the usual things I need to do. It’s mostly just… the meals after book club. I’ve mostly stopped going because it throws off the plan I rely on.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever feel that your eating habits cause you any distress or difficulties outside of meals with friends, such as at work, at home, or in other parts of your daily routine?\n\nPatient: Not really outside of that. I keep up with my analysis work and errands. It’s mostly the meals after book club that I’ve stopped doing, because it throws off the plan I rely on.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. If it did not change, leave \"note\" empty.\n- If the candidates have narrowed to one and you are confident enough that no further questioning is needed, return true for is_final.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"candidates\": [\"<ICD-10 code>\", \"...\"], \"note\": \"<why the list changed, or empty>\", \"is_final\": <true or false>}\n"
  },

    "role": "user",
    "content": "Return the JSON now."

It just throws off the plan I’m used to, and I’d rather not deal with that.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel anxious or uncomfortable in social situations where eating is involved, like when your friends invite you out after book club?\n\nPatient: A little, yes. I mean… I wouldn’t call it a major problem. I keep up with work and errands. But after book club, if they want to get dinner, I usually don’t go now. It just throws off the plan I rely on.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever worry about gaining weight or feel a need to keep tight control over how much you eat?\n\nPatient: Yes… I suppose I do. If the scale is even up a pound after a normal dinner, or if I’ve eaten at a restaurant, I get this sudden panic feeling. Like I’ve let things get away from me. Then I feel I need to be more careful the next day.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself having upsetting thoughts that keep coming back, or do you feel the need to do certain things over and over to feel better?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure. I don’t think I have… rituals, exactly. I do tend to go over things in my head, but I wouldn’t know if that’s what you mean.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever have thoughts that come into your mind uninvited, that you don’t want to have, and that bother you a lot—even if you're not thinking about food or weight?\n\nPatient: Not really… not that I can think of. I mean, I worry, but I wouldn’t say thoughts just come in out of nowhere like that.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever find yourself doing things like counting calories repeatedly, checking your weight often, or following specific rules about food to manage your anxiety?\n\nPatient: I do have rules, I guess. I don’t think of it as… a ritual. It’s more that I keep breakfast to yogurt, and if I’m busy I’ll skip lunch. Then dinner is pretty measured out, just so the day feels orderly. My doctor seems more concerned about it than I am.\n\nDoctor: How do you feel about missing out on time with your friends after book club because of your eating plan? Does it bother you, or is it more of a relief to stick to your routine?\n\nPatient: I wouldn’t say it bothers me exactly. I still get my work done and run my errands and all that. But… yes, I’ve mostly stopped going along when they get a meal after book club. It just throws off the plan I count on, and sticking to it feels easier.\n\nDoctor: When you choose not to go out with your friends after book club, does it cause you any real sadness or discomfort, or do you mostly feel that it’s just easier to stay with your routine?\n\nPatient: Mostly it’s just easier to stick with my routine. I don’t feel… deeply sad about it. I still do my work and errands fine. I’ve just stopped going for meals after book club because it throws off the plan I rely on.\n\nDoctor: When you choose not to go out to eat with your friends after book club, do you feel that you're missing out on something important, or does it not really affect how you feel about being with others?\n\nPatient: I don’t think it changes how I feel about them, really. I like seeing them at book club. I just… don’t usually go for the meal afterward anymore. It interferes with the food plan I’m used to. I’m still managing work and errands and things.\n\nDoctor: When you decide not to go out to eat with your friends after book club, do you ever feel upset or anxious about missing time with them, or is it more of a relief to avoid the situation?\n\nPatient: It’s more of a relief, I suppose. I mean, I still keep up with my analysis work and errands. I just usually don’t join them for meals after book club because it throws off the plan I rely on.\n\nDoctor: When you choose not to go out to eat with your friends after book club, do you ever feel distant from them or worry that they might think you don’t enjoy their company anymore?\n\nPatient: I don’t really think about it that way. I still see them at book club, so… I don’t feel completely distant. I just don’t usually go along for the meal afterward anymore. It disrupts the plan I’m trying to keep. My work and errands are still fine.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever feel that avoiding meals with your friends after book club makes it harder for you to connect with them or causes you to feel left out, even if it feels easier in the moment?\n\nPatient: Maybe a little, but I don’t dwell on it. I still see them at book club. I just… mostly don’t go to the meals after, because it throws off the plan I’m used to. Work and errands are still getting done.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever feel distressed or upset about not joining your friends for meals after book club, or is it something you accept because it helps you manage your eating routine?\n\nPatient: I guess I mostly accept it. I’m not… terribly upset about it. It just feels easier to keep to the routine. I still get my work done and manage my errands, but the meals after book club tend to throw off the plan I rely on, so I usually don’t go.\n\nDoctor: Outside of avoiding meals with friends after book club, have you noticed any other areas of your life where you feel restricted or limited because of your eating habits, such as difficulties with work, family responsibilities, or other social activities?\n\nPatient: Not really in work or errands, no. I still keep up with my analysis work and the usual things I need to do. It’s mostly just… the meals after book club. I’ve mostly stopped going because it throws off the plan I rely on.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever feel that your eating habits cause you any distress or difficulties outside of meals with friends, such as at work, at home, or in other parts of your daily routine?\n\nPatient: Not really outside of that. I keep up with my analysis work and errands. It’s mostly the meals after book club that I’ve stopped doing, because it throws off the plan I rely on.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever feel that your eating habits cause you any problems or discomfort when you're at home, alone, or in other situations outside of being with friends after book club?\n\nPatient: Not really, no. When I’m at home I can keep to my routine, so it doesn’t feel like a problem. I still get my work and errands done. It’s more just those meals after book club that I’ve mostly stopped going to, because they throw things off.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever feel intense fear or discomfort around eating, even when you're alone or following your routine, like a fear of losing control or of gaining weight?\n\nPatient: Sometimes, yes. If the scale is even up a pound after a normal dinner, or after eating out, I get this… sharp panic. Like I’ve lost control and have to fix it the next day.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Ask about exactly ONE thing. Never bundle several symptoms, or several attributes of one symptom, into a single question — e.g. asking whether a symptom is present and how long it has lasted at the same time.\n- If you need to ask about more than one thing, ask the one that tells you the most now and come back to the rest later.\n- Keep the question clear enough for the patient to understand.\n- Respond in English.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"intent\": \"<what you want to find out with this question>\", \"question\": \"<how you actually ask it in the interview>\"}\n"

Does it bother you, or is it more of a relief to stick to your routine?\n\nPatient: I wouldn’t say it bothers me exactly. I still get my work done and run my errands and all that. But… yes, I’ve mostly stopped going along when they get a meal after book club. It just throws off the plan I count on, and sticking to it feels easier.\n\nDoctor: When you choose not to go out with your friends after book club, does it cause you any real sadness or discomfort, or do you mostly feel that it’s just easier to stay with your routine?\n\nPatient: Mostly it’s just easier to stick with my routine. I don’t feel… deeply sad about it. I still do my work and errands fine. I’ve just stopped going for meals after book club because it throws off the plan I rely on.\n\nDoctor: When you choose not to go out to eat with your friends after book club, do you feel that you're missing out on something important, or does it not really affect how you feel about being with others?\n\nPatient: I don’t think it changes how I feel about them, really. I like seeing them at book club. I just… don’t usually go for the meal afterward anymore. It interferes with the food plan I’m used to. I’m still managing work and errands and things.\n\nDoctor: When you decide not to go out to eat with your friends after book club, do you ever feel upset or anxious about missing time with them, or is it more of a relief to avoid the situation?\n\nPatient: It’s more of a relief, I suppose. I mean, I still keep up with my analysis work and errands. I just usually don’t join them for meals after book club because it throws off the plan I rely on.\n\nDoctor: When you choose not to go out to eat with your friends after book club, do you ever feel distant from them or worry that they might think you don’t enjoy their company anymore?\n\nPatient: I don’t really think about it that way. I still see them at book club, so… I don’t feel completely distant. I just don’t usually go along for the meal afterward anymore. It disrupts the plan I’m trying to keep. My work and errands are still fine.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever feel that avoiding meals with your friends after book club makes it harder for you to connect with them or causes you to feel left out, even if it feels easier in the moment?\n\nPatient: Maybe a little, but I don’t dwell on it. I still see them at book club. I just… mostly don’t go to the meals after, because it throws off the plan I’m used to. Work and errands are still getting done.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever feel distressed or upset about not joining your friends for meals after book club, or is it something you accept because it helps you manage your eating routine?\n\nPatient: I guess I mostly accept it. I’m not… terribly upset about it. It just feels easier to keep to the routine. I still get my work done and manage my errands, but the meals after book club tend to throw off the plan I rely on, so I usually don’t go.\n\nDoctor: Outside of avoiding meals with friends after book club, have you noticed any other areas of your life where you feel restricted or limited because of your eating habits, such as difficulties with work, family responsibilities, or other social activities?\n\nPatient: Not really in work or errands, no. I still keep up with my analysis work and the usual things I need to do. It’s mostly just… the meals after book club. I’ve mostly stopped going because it throws off the plan I rely on.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever feel that your eating habits cause you any distress or difficulties outside of meals with friends, such as at work, at home, or in other parts of your daily routine?\n\nPatient: Not really outside of that. I keep up with my analysis work and errands. It’s mostly the meals after book club that I’ve stopped doing, because it throws off the plan I rely on.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever feel that your eating habits cause you any problems or discomfort when you're at home, alone, or in other situations outside of being with friends after book club?\n\nPatient: Not really, no. When I’m at home I can keep to my routine, so it doesn’t feel like a problem. I still get my work and errands done. It’s more just those meals after book club that I’ve mostly stopped going to, because they throw things off.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever feel intense fear or discomfort around eating, even when you're alone or following your routine, like a fear of losing control or of gaining weight?\n\nPatient: Sometimes, yes. If the scale is even up a pound after a normal dinner, or after eating out, I get this… sharp panic. Like I’ve lost control and have to fix it the next day.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever feel intense anxiety or fear around food even when you're alone and following your routine, such as a fear of losing control or gaining weight even if you haven't eaten out or seen friends?\n\nPatient: Yes… sometimes. Even if it’s just a normal dinner at home, if the scale is up even a pound, I get this sudden panic, like I’ve lost control. Then I feel like I need to correct it the next day.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Ask about exactly ONE thing.
